Yeah, um… honestly, I’m here because things at work have gotten kind of out of hand with me forgetting stuff. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, the job pressure makes it more obvious now because people are counting on me and there are consequences, but I can remember being like this way back in grade school.

Teachers would say I was bright, like I understood things when I was paying attention, but I was always losing papers or leaving something in my desk or my backpack and then acting surprised when it was due. Or I’d hear directions and somehow only do part of them, like I missed a step even though I was sitting right there. And I needed a lot of reminders to finish classwork, because I’d start something and then… I don’t know, it would just not get completed unless someone came around and pointed me back to it.

So, yeah, this has been around a long time. It just feels like now, as an adult, there’s less room for messing it up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t think that’s really the main issue for me. I mean, I can get a little antsy if I’m stuck somewhere too long, like anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly restless or bouncing around or unable to sit still.

And the impulsive thing… I don’t know. I don’t think people would describe me as blurting things out all the time or making wild snap decisions. I might talk over someone once in a while if I’m trying to remember what I was about to say before it leaves my head, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always interrupting or acting without thinking.

For me, it’s more like… the problem isn’t that I’m charging ahead too fast, it’s that things slip away from me. But as far as restlessness or impulsive stuff, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big pattern.

But as far as restlessness or impulsive stuff, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big pattern.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and trouble finishing tasks affecting your life outside of work too—like at home, managing bills, or in your personal relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / maintenance_or_repair_worker_general / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I promised people, and now it’s piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up other places too, but the big thing that brought me in is really the work side of it. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even just basic follow-ups I told people I would do. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll check on that and get back to you,” and in the moment I mean it, I’m not blowing them off, but then it just falls out of my head unless there’s something right in front of me forcing me to remember.

And now it’s gotten piled up enough that it’s not just a little embarrassing mistake here and there. It’s affecting how people see me at work, and they basically sent me here because I’m at the point where if I don’t get a handle on it, I could lose my chance at moving up. That’s what scares me, because I’ve worked hard, and I don’t want people thinking I don’t care or I’m being lazy. It’s more like I can’t seem to keep all the little follow-through pieces from slipping away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, the clearest examples are still the work-type things, honestly. That’s what’s been the biggest mess. Like I’ll have a scheduled callback or a work order I’m supposed to follow up on, and I’ll fully intend to do it, and then unless it’s right in front of me or somebody reminds me, it just slips. Then a day or two later I realize, or someone asks me about it, and I feel terrible because I did tell them I’d handle it.

And it’s not one single huge disaster, it’s more that it keeps happening in these little follow-up pieces. Basic stuff I promised people I’d do—check back, call someone, finish a work order, pass along information—and then it piles up. That’s really what brought me in. It’s gotten to the point where work sent me here because they’re worried, and I’m worried too, because I could lose my shot at moving up if I don’t get this under control.

It’s embarrassing, and it’s starting to scare me because it’s not just one little mistake here and there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble with forgetfulness and staying on top of tasks like this since you were a kid or young adult, or is this a new problem that's only started recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, the job pressure makes it more obvious now because people are counting on me and there are consequences, but I can remember being like this way back in grade school.\n\nTeachers would say I was bright, like I understood things when I was paying attention, but I was always losing papers or leaving something in my desk or my backpack and then acting surprised when it was due. Or I’d hear directions and somehow only do part of them, like I missed a step even though I was sitting right there. And I needed a lot of reminders to finish classwork, because I’d start something and then… I don’t know, it would just not get completed unless someone came around and pointed me back to it.\n\nSo, yeah, this has been around a long time. It just feels like now, as an adult, there’s less room for messing it up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the forgetfulness, have you been feeling persistently down, low on energy, or unusually anxious and worried over the past several weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that I’d call it that, no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and embarrassed about the work stuff, definitely, and I’ll worry when I realize I dropped the ball on something because then I’m thinking, “Great, now who did I let down?” But I don’t feel like I’ve been just persistently down for weeks in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nEnergy-wise… I mean, I get tired like anybody does, especially after a full day, but I haven’t noticed some big change where I’m dragging all the time for no reason. And anxious—again, I worry about the consequences when I mess something up, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually anxious all day or worrying about everything. It’s more tied to the stuff I’m not staying on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or find it hard to sit still, or notice yourself acting or speaking impulsively—like interrupting others or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think that’s really the main issue for me. I mean, I can get a little antsy if I’m stuck somewhere too long, like anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly restless or bouncing around or unable to sit still.\n\nAnd the impulsive thing… I don’t know. I don’t think people would describe me as blurting things out all the time or making wild snap decisions. It’s affecting how people see me at work, and they basically sent me here because I’m at the point where if I don’t get a handle on it, I could lose my chance at moving up. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Honestly, I’m having a hard time coming up with a clean home example that isn’t kind of connected back to work, because that’s where it’s really blowing up right now. I know you’re asking separate from work, and I’m not trying to dodge it, it’s just… the thing that’s been causing the real problem is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and these basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do.

Like, I’ll tell someone, “Yes, I’ll get back to you,” and in my head I really believe I’m going to do it. It’s not that I don’t care. But then it’s gone unless something is right in front of me reminding me. And now there have been enough of those that it’s not just one little mistake anymore. It’s piled up, and work actually sent me here because they’re worried about it. I’m worried too, because I could lose my chance at moving up over this.

So… personal life specifically, I can’t think of one big recent story the same way. The real problem that brought me in is the work follow-through stuff getting so bad that it’s affecting my future there.

I mean, the job pressure makes it more obvious now because people are counting on me and there are consequences, but I can remember being like this way back in grade school.\n\nTeachers would say I was bright, like I understood things when I was paying attention, but I was always losing papers or leaving something in my desk or my backpack and then acting surprised when it was due. Or I’d hear directions and somehow only do part of them, like I missed a step even though I was sitting right there. It just feels like now, as an adult, there’s less room for messing it up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the forgetfulness, have you been feeling persistently down, low on energy, or unusually anxious and worried over the past several weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that I’d call it that, no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and embarrassed about the work stuff, definitely, and I’ll worry when I realize I dropped the ball on something because then I’m thinking, “Great, now who did I let down?” But I don’t feel like I’ve been just persistently down for weeks in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nEnergy-wise… I mean, I get tired like anybody does, especially after a full day, but I haven’t noticed some big change where I’m dragging all the time for no reason. And anxious—again, I worry about the consequences when I mess something up, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually anxious all day or worrying about everything. It’s more tied to the stuff I’m not staying on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or find it hard to sit still, or notice yourself acting or speaking impulsively—like interrupting others or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think that’s really the main issue for me. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and trouble finishing tasks affecting your life outside of work too—like at home, managing bills, or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up other places too, but the big thing that brought me in is really the work side of it. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even just basic follow-ups I told people I would do. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll check on that and get back to you,” and in the moment I mean it, I’m not blowing them off, but then it just falls out of my head unless there’s something right in front of me forcing me to remember.\n\nAnd now it’s gotten piled up enough that it’s not just a little embarrassing mistake here and there. It’s affecting how people see me at work, and they basically sent me here because I’m at the point where if I don’t get a handle on it, I could lose my chance at moving up. That’s what scares me, because I’ve worked hard, and I don’t want people thinking I don’t care or I’m being lazy. It’s more like I can’t seem to keep all the little follow-through pieces from slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of how this forgetfulness has caused problems at home—like missing bill payments, forgetting appointments, or letting down family members?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest examples are still the work-type things, honestly. That’s what’s been the biggest mess. Like I’ll have a scheduled callback or a work order I’m supposed to follow up on, and I’ll fully intend to do it, and then unless it’s right in front of me or somebody reminds me, it just slips. Then a day or two later I realize, or someone asks me about it, and I feel terrible because I did tell them I’d handle it.\n\nAnd it’s not one single huge disaster, it’s more that it keeps happening in these little follow-up pieces. Basic stuff I promised people I’d do—check back, call someone, finish a work order, pass along information—and then it piles up. That’s really what brought me in. It’s gotten to the point where work sent me here because they’re worried, and I’m worried too, because I could lose my shot at moving up if I don’t get this under control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about a specific time recently when this forgetfulness caused a real problem at home or in your personal life, separate from work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m having a hard time coming up with a clean home example that isn’t kind of connected back to work, because that’s where it’s really blowing up right now. I know you’re asking separate from work, and I’m not trying to dodge it, it’s just… the thing that’s been causing the real problem is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and these basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do.\n\nLike, I’ll tell someone, “Yes, I’ll get back to you,” and in my head I really believe I’m going to do it. It’s not that I don’t care. But then it’s gone unless something is right in front of me reminding me. And now there have been enough of those that it’s not just one little mistake anymore. It’s piled up, and work actually sent me here because they’re worried about it. I’m worried too, because I could lose my chance at moving up over this.\n\nSo… personal life specifically, I can’t think of one big recent story the same way. The real problem that brought me in is the work follow-through stuff getting so bad that it’s affecting my future there.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of work, do you have trouble with things like paying bills on time, keeping track of appointments, or finishing household tasks you start—even if nothing bad has happened yet, do you notice yourself struggling to stay on top of these things at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / maintenance_or_repair_worker_general / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I promised people, and now it’s piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to get at whether it’s just a work thing or if it’s everywhere. I guess I keep circling back because the work examples are the ones that are right in my face and causing actual consequences right now.

The main thing is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care or I’m deciding not to do them. I’ll fully mean it when I say, “I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” or “I’ll finish that order,” and then it just disappears on me unless something is staring me in the face.

At home, I’m not thinking of a specific bill or appointment example that’s caused a big problem, but I do feel like the same kind of “keeping track of everything” is hard for me. It’s just that the thing that brought me in, and the thing that’s gotten bad enough that other people are noticing, is work. It’s piled up so much that they sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up, and honestly that’s what scared me enough to come.

That’s what’s been the biggest mess. It’s gotten to the point where work sent me here because they’re worried, and I’m worried too, because I could lose my shot at moving up if I don’t get this under control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about a specific time recently when this forgetfulness caused a real problem at home or in your personal life, separate from work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m having a hard time coming up with a clean home example that isn’t kind of connected back to work, because that’s where it’s really blowing up right now. I know you’re asking separate from work, and I’m not trying to dodge it, it’s just… the thing that’s been causing the real problem is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and these basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do.\n\nLike, I’ll tell someone, “Yes, I’ll get back to you,” and in my head I really believe I’m going to do it. It’s piled up, and work actually sent me here because they’re worried about it. The real problem that brought me in is the work follow-through stuff getting so bad that it’s affecting my future there.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble with things like paying bills on time, keeping track of appointments, or finishing household tasks you start—even if nothing bad has happened yet, do you notice yourself struggling to stay on top of these things at home?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to get at whether it’s just a work thing or if it’s everywhere. I guess I keep circling back because the work examples are the ones that are right in my face and causing actual consequences right now.\n\nThe main thing is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care or I’m deciding not to do them. I’ll fully mean it when I say, “I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” or “I’ll finish that order,” and then it just disappears on me unless something is staring me in the face.\n\nAt home, I’m not thinking of a specific bill or appointment example that’s caused a big problem, but I do feel like the same kind of “keeping track of everything” is hard for me. It’s just that the thing that brought me in, and the thing that’s gotten bad enough that other people are noticing, is work. It’s piled up so much that they sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up, and honestly that’s what scared me enough to come.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave people close to you—like a partner, friend, or family member—ever told you that your forgetfulness or trouble finishing things has affected your relationship with them, even in small ways?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I mean, the job pressure makes it more obvious now because people are counting on me and there are consequences, but I can remember being like this way back in grade school.\n\nTeachers would say I was bright, like I understood things when I was paying attention, but I was always losing papers or leaving something in my desk or my backpack and then acting surprised when it was due. It just feels like now, as an adult, there’s less room for messing it up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the forgetfulness, have you been feeling persistently down, low on energy, or unusually anxious and worried over the past several weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that I’d call it that, no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and embarrassed about the work stuff, definitely, and I’ll worry when I realize I dropped the ball on something because then I’m thinking, “Great, now who did I let down?” But I don’t feel like I’ve been just persistently down for weeks in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nEnergy-wise… I mean, I get tired like anybody does, especially after a full day, but I haven’t noticed some big change where I’m dragging all the time for no reason. And anxious—again, I worry about the consequences when I mess something up, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually anxious all day or worrying about everything. It’s more tied to the stuff I’m not staying on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or find it hard to sit still, or notice yourself acting or speaking impulsively—like interrupting others or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think that’s really the main issue for me. I mean, I can get a little antsy if I’m stuck somewhere too long, like anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly restless or bouncing around or unable to sit still.\n\nAnd the impulsive thing… I don’t know. I don’t think people would describe me as blurting things out all the time or making wild snap decisions. I might talk over someone once in a while if I’m trying to remember what I was about to say before it leaves my head, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always interrupting or acting without thinking.\n\nFor me, it’s more like… the problem isn’t that I’m charging ahead too fast, it’s that things slip away from me. But as far as restlessness or impulsive stuff, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and trouble finishing tasks affecting your life outside of work too—like at home, managing bills, or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up other places too, but the big thing that brought me in is really the work side of it. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even just basic follow-ups I told people I would do. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll check on that and get back to you,” and in the moment I mean it, I’m not blowing them off, but then it just falls out of my head unless there’s something right in front of me forcing me to remember.\n\nAnd now it’s gotten piled up enough that it’s not just a little embarrassing mistake here and there. It’s affecting how people see me at work, and they basically sent me here because I’m at the point where if I don’t get a handle on it, I could lose my chance at moving up. It’s more like I can’t seem to keep all the little follow-through pieces from slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of how this forgetfulness has caused problems at home—like missing bill payments, forgetting appointments, or letting down family members?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest examples are still the work-type things, honestly. But as far as restlessness or impulsive stuff, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and trouble finishing tasks affecting your life outside of work too—like at home, managing bills, or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up other places too, but the big thing that brought me in is really the work side of it. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even just basic follow-ups I told people I would do. Like I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll check on that and get back to you,” and in the moment I mean it, I’m not blowing them off, but then it just falls out of my head unless there’s something right in front of me forcing me to remember.\n\nAnd now it’s gotten piled up enough that it’s not just a little embarrassing mistake here and there. It’s affecting how people see me at work, and they basically sent me here because I’m at the point where if I don’t get a handle on it, I could lose my chance at moving up. That’s what scares me, because I’ve worked hard, and I don’t want people thinking I don’t care or I’m being lazy. It’s more like I can’t seem to keep all the little follow-through pieces from slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of how this forgetfulness has caused problems at home—like missing bill payments, forgetting appointments, or letting down family members?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest examples are still the work-type things, honestly. That’s what’s been the biggest mess. Like I’ll have a scheduled callback or a work order I’m supposed to follow up on, and I’ll fully intend to do it, and then unless it’s right in front of me or somebody reminds me, it just slips. Then a day or two later I realize, or someone asks me about it, and I feel terrible because I did tell them I’d handle it.\n\nAnd it’s not one single huge disaster, it’s more that it keeps happening in these little follow-up pieces. Basic stuff I promised people I’d do—check back, call someone, finish a work order, pass along information—and then it piles up. That’s really what brought me in. It’s gotten to the point where work sent me here because they’re worried, and I’m worried too, because I could lose my shot at moving up if I don’t get this under control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about a specific time recently when this forgetfulness caused a real problem at home or in your personal life, separate from work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m having a hard time coming up with a clean home example that isn’t kind of connected back to work, because that’s where it’s really blowing up right now. I know you’re asking separate from work, and I’m not trying to dodge it, it’s just… the thing that’s been causing the real problem is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and these basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do.\n\nLike, I’ll tell someone, “Yes, I’ll get back to you,” and in my head I really believe I’m going to do it. It’s not that I don’t care. But then it’s gone unless something is right in front of me reminding me. And now there have been enough of those that it’s not just one little mistake anymore. It’s piled up, and work actually sent me here because they’re worried about it. I’m worried too, because I could lose my chance at moving up over this.\n\nSo… personal life specifically, I can’t think of one big recent story the same way. The real problem that brought me in is the work follow-through stuff getting so bad that it’s affecting my future there.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble with things like paying bills on time, keeping track of appointments, or finishing household tasks you start—even if nothing bad has happened yet, do you notice yourself struggling to stay on top of these things at home?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to get at whether it’s just a work thing or if it’s everywhere. I guess I keep circling back because the work examples are the ones that are right in my face and causing actual consequences right now.\n\nThe main thing is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care or I’m deciding not to do them. I’ll fully mean it when I say, “I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” or “I’ll finish that order,” and then it just disappears on me unless something is staring me in the face.\n\nAt home, I’m not thinking of a specific bill or appointment example that’s caused a big problem, but I do feel like the same kind of “keeping track of everything” is hard for me. It’s piled up so much that they sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up, and honestly that’s what scared me enough to come.\n\nDoctor: Have people close to you—like a partner, friend, or family member—ever told you that your forgetfulness or trouble finishing things has affected your relationship with them, even in small ways?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I do, but honestly the examples that come to mind still look kind of like maintenance-type stuff, because that’s just how my brain frames “projects,” I guess.

Like I’ll start something and I can get the main part done — the actual fix, the hands-on part. If something is broken, I can figure it out, take it apart, repair it, replace the piece, whatever it needs. But then the last little steps are where I kind of fall off. I’ll finish the repair and then somehow not close out the ticket, or I’ll forget to label the panel after I’ve been in it, or I’ll mean to check back with the tenant and make sure everything is still working and then that part just… doesn’t happen unless something reminds me.

And that’s frustrating, because it’s not like the whole job didn’t get done. The important physical fix might be done. But then there are these follow-through pieces at the end that matter, and I leave them hanging. It makes me look careless, even though I don’t feel careless when I’m doing it. I’ll be focused on the repair itself, and then once that’s handled, my brain acts like the task is over, even though there are still steps left.

It’s not that I don’t care. But then it’s gone unless something is right in front of me reminding me. And now there have been enough of those that it’s not just one little mistake anymore. It’s piled up, and work actually sent me here because they’re worried about it. I’m worried too, because I could lose my chance at moving up over this.\n\nSo… personal life specifically, I can’t think of one big recent story the same way. The real problem that brought me in is the work follow-through stuff getting so bad that it’s affecting my future there.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble with things like paying bills on time, keeping track of appointments, or finishing household tasks you start—even if nothing bad has happened yet, do you notice yourself struggling to stay on top of these things at home?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to get at whether it’s just a work thing or if it’s everywhere. I guess I keep circling back because the work examples are the ones that are right in my face and causing actual consequences right now.\n\nThe main thing is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do. And it’s not like I don’t care or I’m deciding not to do them. I’ll fully mean it when I say, “I’ll call them back,” or “I’ll check on that,” or “I’ll finish that order,” and then it just disappears on me unless something is staring me in the face.\n\nAt home, I’m not thinking of a specific bill or appointment example that’s caused a big problem, but I do feel like the same kind of “keeping track of everything” is hard for me. It’s just that the thing that brought me in, and the thing that’s gotten bad enough that other people are noticing, is work. It’s piled up so much that they sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up, and honestly that’s what scared me enough to come.\n\nDoctor: Have people close to you—like a partner, friend, or family member—ever told you that your forgetfulness or trouble finishing things has affected your relationship with them, even in small ways?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can’t think of somebody close to me sitting me down and saying, like, “You’re hurting our relationship because you forget things,” or anything that clear.\n\nI mean, people probably get annoyed with me sometimes, I’m sure. But I’m trying not to just make something up. The main thing I can point to, where people are actually telling me there’s a problem, is still work. That’s where the follow-through stuff has become obvious enough that people are noticing and it’s causing consequences.\n\nWith family or friends, I can’t think of a specific example like that right now. Maybe if you asked them, they’d have their own version of it, but from my side I don’t have a clear one to give you.\n\nDoctor: Think about hobbies or personal projects you start on your own time—do you often find yourself losing track of steps or not finishing them, even when no one else is involved or reminding you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, but honestly the examples that come to mind still look kind of like maintenance-type stuff, because that’s just how my brain frames “projects,” I guess.\n\nLike I’ll start something and I can get the main part done — the actual fix, the hands-on part. If something is broken, I can figure it out, take it apart, repair it, replace the piece, whatever it needs. But then the last little steps are where I kind of fall off. I’ll finish the repair and then somehow not close out the ticket, or I’ll forget to label the panel after I’ve been in it, or I’ll mean to check back with the tenant and make sure everything is still working and then that part just… doesn’t happen unless something reminds me.\n\nAnd that’s frustrating, because it’s not like the whole job didn’t get done. The important physical fix might be done. But then there are these follow-through pieces at the end that matter, and I leave them hanging. It makes me look careless, even though I don’t feel careless when I’m doing it. I’ll be focused on the repair itself, and then once that’s handled, my brain acts like the task is over, even though there are still steps left.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I don’t have a clear memory of being described that way as a kid, like nobody in my family that I remember was saying, “She can’t sit still,” or “She’s bouncing off the walls,” or anything like that.

I mean, it’s been a long time, so I don’t want to say absolutely no, because I may just not remember, and my parents or teachers might have had their own opinion back then. But from what I can pull up, I don’t think I was known as the hyper kid or the impulsive kid. I don’t have a specific story of getting in trouble for running around or not staying in my seat.

So I guess my answer is I’m not sure, but nothing obvious comes to mind for that.

It’s embarrassing, and it’s starting to scare me because it’s not just one little mistake here and there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble with forgetfulness and staying on top of tasks like this since you were a kid or young adult, or is this a new problem that's only started recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, the job pressure makes it more obvious now because people are counting on me and there are consequences, but I can remember being like this way back in grade school.\n\nTeachers would say I was bright, like I understood things when I was paying attention, but I was always losing papers or leaving something in my desk or my backpack and then acting surprised when it was due. Or I’d hear directions and somehow only do part of them, like I missed a step even though I was sitting right there. And I needed a lot of reminders to finish classwork, because I’d start something and then… I don’t know, it would just not get completed unless someone came around and pointed me back to it.\n\nSo, yeah, this has been around a long time. It just feels like now, as an adult, there’s less room for messing it up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the forgetfulness, have you been feeling persistently down, low on energy, or unusually anxious and worried over the past several weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that I’d call it that, no. And anxious—again, I worry about the consequences when I mess something up, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually anxious all day or worrying about everything. It’s more tied to the stuff I’m not staying on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or find it hard to sit still, or notice yourself acting or speaking impulsively—like interrupting others or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think that’s really the main issue for me. I mean, I can get a little antsy if I’m stuck somewhere too long, like anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly restless or bouncing around or unable to sit still.\n\nAnd the impulsive thing… I don’t know. But as far as restlessness or impulsive stuff, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and trouble finishing tasks affecting your life outside of work too—like at home, managing bills, or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s showing up other places too, but the big thing that brought me in is really the work side of it. I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even just basic follow-ups I told people I would do. That’s what’s been the biggest mess. Like I’ll have a scheduled callback or a work order I’m supposed to follow up on, and I’ll fully intend to do it, and then unless it’s right in front of me or somebody reminds me, it just slips. Then a day or two later I realize, or someone asks me about it, and I feel terrible because I did tell them I’d handle it.\n\nAnd it’s not one single huge disaster, it’s more that it keeps happening in these little follow-up pieces. Basic stuff I promised people I’d do—check back, call someone, finish a work order, pass along information—and then it piles up. That’s really what brought me in. It’s gotten to the point where work sent me here because they’re worried, and I’m worried too, because I could lose my shot at moving up if I don’t get this under control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about a specific time recently when this forgetfulness caused a real problem at home or in your personal life, separate from work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m having a hard time coming up with a clean home example that isn’t kind of connected back to work, because that’s where it’s really blowing up right now. I know you’re asking separate from work, and I’m not trying to dodge it, it’s just… the thing that’s been causing the real problem is that I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and these basic follow-ups I already promised people I’d do.\n\nLike, I’ll tell someone, “Yes, I’ll get back to you,” and in my head I really believe I’m going to do it. It’s not that I don’t care. But then it’s gone unless something is right in front of me reminding me. And now there have been enough of those that it’s not just one little mistake anymore. It’s piled up, and work actually sent me here because they’re worried about it. I’m worried too, because I could lose my chance at moving up over this.\n\nSo… personal life specifically, I can’t think of one big recent story the same way. The real problem that brought me in is the work follow-through stuff getting so bad that it’s affecting my future there.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble with things like paying bills on time, keeping track of appointments, or finishing household tasks you start—even if nothing bad has happened yet, do you notice yourself struggling to stay on top of these things at home?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to get at whether it’s just a work thing or if it’s everywhere. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t really know that I’d call it that, no. I mean, I’ve been stressed and embarrassed about the work stuff, definitely, and I’ll worry when I realize I dropped the ball on something because then I’m thinking, “Great, now who did I let down?” But I don’t feel like I’ve been just persistently down for weeks in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nEnergy-wise… I mean, I get tired like anybody does, especially after a full day, but I haven’t noticed some big change where I’m dragging all the time for no reason. It’s more tied to the stuff I’m not staying on top of.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah… I think I do, but honestly the examples that come to mind still look kind of like maintenance-type stuff, because that’s just how my brain frames “projects,” I guess.\n\nLike I’ll start something and I can get the main part done — the actual fix, the hands-on part. If something is broken, I can figure it out, take it apart, repair it, replace the piece, whatever it needs. But then the last little steps are where I kind of fall off. I’ll finish the repair and then somehow not close out the ticket, or I’ll forget to label the panel after I’ve been in it, or I’ll mean to check back with the tenant and make sure everything is still working and then that part just… doesn’t happen unless something reminds me.\n\nAnd that’s frustrating, because it’s not like the whole job didn’t get done. The important physical fix might be done. But then there are these follow-through pieces at the end that matter, and I leave them hanging. It makes me look careless, even though I don’t feel careless when I’m doing it. I’ll be focused on the repair itself, and then once that’s handled, my brain acts like the task is over, even though there are still steps left.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
